HOLBROOK, AZ 86025
One or more supervisors may attend telephonically
Pursuant to A.R.S. §38-431.02(H), the public body will have physical access to the meeting place one (1) hour prior to the start of
the meeting. The Board may vote to meet in a closed executive session to discuss certain matters and for legal advice on any item
as indicated on the following agenda, pursuant to A.R.S. § 38-431.03(A)(3). Items on the agenda may be considered out of order at
the Chairperson's discretion. A copy of the agenda background material provided to the Board, except items to be considered in the
executive session, is available for public inspection at the Navajo County website, www.navajocountyaz.gov 
WATCH THE MEETING LIVE AT: www.navajocountyaz.gov/660
All public comments will need to be made in person or in writing. Written comments will be received by the Clerk of the Board's
office twenty-four (24) hours prior to the Board meeting, at melissa.buckley@navajocountyaz.gov. Persons with disabilities who
need accommodation to attend or participate in the meeting may contact the Clerk of the Board’s office at least forty-eight (48)
hours prior to the meeting, so accommodation can be arranged.

website and social media pages. If you permit your child to attend/participate in a televised Navajo County Board of Supervisors meeting, a recording will be made, and

in the meeting.”

a. Consideration of an Intergovernmental Agreement with the Town of

financial assistance for the extension of the 7th South Street Project in the
amount of $120,000, motion seconded by Supervisor Seymore.  Vote
unanimous approving the motion.

PLANNING & DEVELOPMENT SERVICES: 
 
a. PUBLIC HEARING: Consideration and adoption of Resolution Number
35-2024 approving/denying a Master Development Plan Amendment for
an approved, approximately 80-unit, single-family residential subdivision to
be amended to allow for 49 single-family lots, 74 townhome units, and
changes to the roadways as well as open spaces in the Starlight Ridge
Estates Special Development within the Wagon Wheel area:
Cody Cooper noted that this item was previously tabled by the Board on
August 27, 2024 to ensure that water supply with the City of Show Low
was adequate and guaranteed and that the traffic concerns had been
handled appropriately for any required routing and infrastructure
improvements.  He stated that the developer has provided an updated
traffic report and has updated will serve from Show Low.  He noted that
this did go back before the Planning and Zoning Commission on April 17,
2025, at which time they unanimously recommended approval with the
amended and updated documents, which are included in the packet.  A
presentation was shown.  Mr. Cooper presented an update on the
proposed amendment to the Starlight Ridge Estates Unit 3 Master
Development Plan, located approximately 1.5 miles west of State Route
260, White Mountain Blvd, noting that units 1 and 2 have already been
platted and partially developed.  He indicated that the project spans 53
acres, that it is already zoned for special development, that it is approved
for a maximum of 80 single-family units, which remains vested, and that
  
                                                                    5 of 15 
DRAFT

<!-- page 12 -->
for a maximum of 80 single-family units, which remains vested, and that
the proposed amendment would be 49 single-family lots and 74 town
homes.  He indicated this is only approving the new usages and densities,
but that there would still need to be a tentative and final plat and further
reports accepted by the county before moving forward with any
development.  He stated that the plan is divided into two phases, phase 1
includes the single-family homes and phase 2 would be the townhomes.
Mr. Cooper provided an update on the site plan, traffic study, and the
will-serve letter from the City of Show Low confirming water service
availability. He indicated that concerns previously raised about Rim Road
access have been addressed through the revised traffic study, which has
been reviewed and tentatively accepted by County Engineering.  He stated
that Planning and Development recommends approval of the amended
Master Development Plan with 11 conditions, including submission and
acceptance of a final traffic report and associated infrastructure
improvements. He indicated that as of August 7, 2024, 13 public
comments in opposition were received, primarily regarding drainage and
traffic, and that no additional comments have been received, to date.
Emery Ellsworth with Painted Sky Engineering, indicated that the goal is to
make the project more financially feasible while addressing the region’s
housing needs. He stated that they have worked closely with County
Planning and Zoning and Engineering for a few years, incorporating
feedback and revising plans accordingly. He indicated that the updated
design aims to minimize impact on surrounding single-family areas while
adding much-needed housing to the community.
 
Chairman Whiting opened the meeting for public comments.  There
being no comments, the public hearing was closed. 
 
Supervisor Seymore inquired if this is the same developer as Starlight
Ridge Estates Unit 1 and 2. 
Mr. Cooper indicated that it is.
Supervisor Seymore expressed concerns regarding the longevity and
quality of the road construction in previous phases, noting the need for
improved inspection and oversight to avoid premature repairs.  He shared
additional concerns about the construction quality and materials used in
earlier units, noting that as the project moves into building townhomes and
condominiums, they need to be more quality than what was previously
built. He encouraged higher standards for future phases and emphasized
the importance of quality open space and amenities. He indicated that the
community needs housing, especially townhomes.  He expressed his
appreciation for the work done in revising and advancing the project.
Vice-Chair Whitesinger inquired about the responsibility for roadway
maintenance outside the 53-acre development, particularly regarding
traffic mitigation and developer obligations.
Mr. Cooper clarified that while offsite roads fall under county maintenance,
any improvements required due to the project must be financially covered
by the developer and bonded in advance.
Vice-Chair Whitesinger spoke about the concerns from nearby residents
and emphasized the importance of building partnerships, including with
tribal communities, especially where infrastructure may impact tribal lands.

changes to the roadways as well as open spaces in the Starlight Ridge
Estates Special Development within the Wagon Wheel area, motion
seconded by Supervisor Seymore.  Vote unanimous approving the
motion. 
 
b. PUBLIC HEARING: Consideration and adoption of Resolution Number
17-2025, approving/denying a comprehensive update to the Navajo
County Zoning Ordinance:
Cody Cooper stated that over the past year staff received significant
community feedback regarding renewable energy and existing renewable
energy ordinances, noting that the Board adopted a new Comprehensive
Plan in January, which recommended a full zoning ordinance update,
including renewable energy regulations.  He spoke about the proposed
updates to the Comprehensive Zoning Ordinance.  He reviewed the
revisions to the zoning districts which updated administrative procedures,
code enforcement, and compliance processes, clear lists of permitted and
special permitted land uses, updated development standards and
definitions, and countywide standards and clarified language throughout. 
He indicated that key improvements included
zoning districts being simplified by removing complex cross-referencing
between zones by consolidating use tables and rural districts being
combined with a unified use list and separate development standard
charts.  He stated that administrative procedures would be streamlined,
noting that violations, enforcement, and penalties were previously spread
across multiple sections would now be consolidated into one article,
Article 21, and that users can now clearly understand what constitutes a
violation, how it’s enforced, and potential penalties.  He spoke about the
land use clarifications, including clear distinctions being added for
permitted, administrative, and special use permits, and administrative
permit requirements now being listed within zoning articles to avoid
confusion during the permitting process.  He spoke about the general
Provisions & Development Standards which include items like yards, open
spaces, and height regulations, being moved to simplified charts for easier
interpretation.  He indicated that special development zoning would be
expanded to include all development types (e.g., site plans, master plans,
tentative/final plats), noting that process expectations and submittal
checklists were added to improve transparency and reduce planning
delays.  He further indicated that parking requirements would be simplified
into a chart and aligned with residential and building codes to avoid
conflicts and plan revisions.  He stated that the sign ordinance would be
updated to comply with Reed v. Town of Gilbert Supreme Court ruling
and that all sign regulations would be content-neutral to avoid First
Amendment violations.
Chairman Whiting shared his appreciation for the efforts to view the
zoning ordinance updates through the lens of the citizens by focusing on
  
                                                                    7 of 15 
DRAFT

<!-- page 14 -->
clarity, accessibility, and usability. He thanked staff for proactively working
to simplify processes, reduce confusion, and better serve the public.
Mr. Cooper stated that as part of this zoning ordinance update, staff
significantly streamlined the document, reducing it from 180 pages to 100,
without cutting meaningful content. He indicated that the goal was to
simplify and condense information to make it more accessible and
user-friendly for the public, noting that they aimed to remove barriers and
create a more approachable and consumable resource for the
community.
Mr. Cooper reviewed the proposed revisions to the renewable energy
ordinance.  He spoke about the integration of wind and solar standards
which would allow for unified standards under renewable energy
development, cover items like decommissioning, bonding, and road use
agreements and would apply to both wind and solar.  He spoke about
Development Agreements and  performance standards which would allow
for development agreements alongside or instead of special use permits
and would include preference criteria for lower-impact or reclaimed land
projects.  He further spoke about Fire Department involvement, indicating
that they hosted listening sessions, resulting in language that now meets
their needs and clarifies roles in permitting and inspections.  As to road
use and maintenance, he indicated that there would be a requirement for
developers to maintain roads during and post-construction and that there
would be road condition assessments required before and after the
project.  He spoke about Municipal & Tribal Coordination, noting that
developers must coordinate with entities within 5 miles of project
boundaries, which is aimed to increase awareness and transparency.  He
spoke about technology and safety requirements, that would mandate
companies to use the best commercially proven technology, to minimize
wildlife impacts.  He further spoke about the mandate for site studies,
including environmental, cultural, wetland, and photo simulation studies. 
He spoke about wind-specific revisions, that would allow for waivers from
neighboring properties within setbacks and preserves property owners'
right to negotiate.  He reviewed the solar-specific revisions, noting that
compliance with zoning setbacks are required and that waiver and
screening provisions were added. Mr. Cooper spoke about the public
engagement, noting that there was extensive public feedback, including
21 comments in opposition to the staff report related to renewal energy
prior to the development of the ordinance, 51 online submissions, 5
letters/emails, and over 80 comments from the 3 public listening sessions. 
He indicated that 7 additional comments were received since the release
of the draft ordinance.  He stated that common concerns included
clarifying language within the ordinance, noise and setback standards for
wind energy, excessive public outreach standards, roll of fire within review,
transference of Special Use Permits requiring hearings for renewable
energy and decommissioning procedures.  He indicated that some of the
items have not been addressed, noting that some items have existed since
the ordinance was first adopted.  He reviewed some of the modifications
that have been made.  He spoke about proposed minor clarifying edits
which include adding “desktop” in front of wetland delineation report, as
well as the cultural resources report to clarify level of detail they expect;
correcting language to include public outreach will be required regardless
                                                                    8 of 15 
DRAFT

<!-- page 15 -->
of the process used; and changing “consult” to “coordinate” with
municipalities/tribes to avoid implying federal-level consultation.  He
clarified some points, noting that solar panels are not considered
impervious surfaces because they allow water to run off and do not
completely cover the ground, so they aren't treated like buildings when
calculating lot coverage. He indicated that the ordinance also allows for
fencing up to eight feet high in residential areas tied to renewable energy
projects, which is more typical of commercial or industrial standards,
noting that it is intentional, not an oversight, as the more specific
renewable energy provisions take precedence over general residential
regulations. Mr. Cooper stated that staff would recommend approval of the
Renewable Energy Ordinance Update, noting that the Planning & Zoning
Commission unanimously recommended approval at their last meeting,
with the three minor clarifying amendments.
Chairman Whiting opened the meeting for public comments.
Tim Grubbs expressed support for the Renewable Energy Ordinance and
commended staff for their well-researched work.  He stated that he is a
supporter of renewable energy, noting that it is clean, viable, and timely
source to meet Arizona’s growing energy demand. He indicated that while
the ordinance may be tough on developers in parts, it reflects public input
and balances economic diversification with environmental benefits.
Don Fogle shared personal observations of climate change and worsening
environmental conditions over the decades. He expressed strong support
for clean, renewable energy sources like wind and solar, emphasizing their
environmental and economic benefits, including local tax revenue, job
creation, and additional income for farmers and ranchers for their land use.
He shared his support for renewable energy development in Navajo
County.
Samantha Cloud stated that she is a Tucson resident but that her family is
longtime property owners in Navajo County.  She shared her support for
renewable energy, indicating that it helps maintain air quality, deals with
rising heat, mitigates some drought issues, and is crucial for water
conservation. She requested that the Board rely on facts, not
misinformation, and emphasized their responsibility as stewards of both
the land and public interest.  She indicated that wind and solar projects
show a commitment to the future and shows that the county cares for the
land and communities.
There being no further comments, the public hearing was closed.
Supervisor Whitesinger stated that this has been a long and thorough
process of developing the ordinance, noting the value of public input, fire
district involvement, and partnership with local stakeholders. She thanked
staff for working to make the ordinance clear, user-friendly, and supportive
of both renewable energy and broader development. She expressed her
gratitude to staff and the public for their contributions to a more accessible,
comprehensive document that balances industry needs with community
concerns.
Supervisor Seymore thanked staff and all involved for their hard work on
the ordinance update, noting that it was a monumental task. He indicated
that the development agreements are a flexible option.  He acknowledged
both public input and the developers' patience during the process. He
spoke about the region’s natural advantages for wind and solar and the
                                                                    9 of 15

<!-- page 16 -->
improvements with the decommissioning provisions. He spoke about
long-term community benefits and planning for the future.
Supervisor Benally thanked staff for their hard work and research on the
ordinance. She shared her appreciation for being informed about both
general planning efforts and the renewable energy components.
Chairman Whiting shared his appreciation for the extensive work and
public engagement on the ordinance, emphasizing that it addressed
citizens' concerns while balancing property rights, public safety, and
responsible governance. He spoke about the personal feelings with
renewable energy but noted the importance of principle over emotion in
decision-making. He thanked staff, the Board, and the public for their input
and commitment throughout the process.
Supervisor Whitesinger made a motion to adopt Resolution Number
17-2025, approving a comprehensive update to the Navajo County Zoning
Ordinance, motion seconded by Supervisor Peshlakai.  Vote unanimous
approving the motion.
 
COUNTY BUSINESS UPDATE: Report from County Manager, County Attorney
and Board members :
Supervisor Seymore thanked Navajo County staff for their responsiveness and
professionalism, sharing recent examples of positive community feedback. He
spoke about the importance of kindness, service, and gratitude, both in county
operations and in national values.  He shared his appreciation for Independence
Day and those who serve the country.
Supervisor Benally reported on attending various meetings.  She stated that
there is a fire near Navajo Mountain that is approximately 2,300 acres, noting that
they believe it started from lightning, and indicated that she has been out a few
times to provide support.  She spoke about attending a road coordination meeting
with NDOT and BIA to discuss bridge and road projects noting that there will be a
request for material for those projects. She indicated that the gravel project on
8065 has been completed.
Vice-Chair Whitesinger shared concerns about the pending Big Beautiful Bill in

County. She spoke about the possible consequences, including staff and service
reductions at Summit, increased uncompensated care, higher uninsured rates,
worse health outcomes, and economic disruption. She indicated that approximately
60,000 residents could be affected. She urged outreach to legislators to advocate
for the county’s healthcare needs.

awareness on the Medicaid issue, especially regarding its potential impact on
Summit Healthcare and uninsured patients. He shared his appreciation for fellow
Board members and their collaboration.  He spoke about the upcoming Fourth of
July celebrations, encouraging responsible and patriotic observance.

ADJOURN:  10:53 a.m. meeting adjourned. 
 
WORK SESSION: At 11:10 a.m. the work session began.  All of the Board of
Supervisors were present. 
 
a. FY26 Tribal Transportation Projects:
Chairman Whiting thanked everyone for being present. Rochelle Lacapa
stated that this work session’s goal is to encourage strategic budgeting for
tribal transportation projects funding Highway User Revenue Funds. She
stated that since many of these projects are made successful through
partnerships with tribal transportation departments and the Bureau of
Indian Affairs, this session will aim to inform and give context to the
County’s procurement process, provide an overview of characteristics of
successful partnerships and breakdown annual HURF allocations. Nicole
Reske discussed that the priority is understanding your budget
responsibilities, including clearly defining the county’s financial contribution
and ensuring it aligns with the available budget capacity. She stated that it
is important to avoid overcommitting to projects beyond your financial
limits. She requested that focus be placed on identifying projects that
exceed the $100,000 threshold, as these may streamline the budgeting
process and help avoid delays or unspent funds. She stated that priority
should be given to shovel-ready projects that align with both county and
partner goals, helping to prevent redundant spending on previously
completed surveys or studies. Lastly, she requested that each project
should have a clearly defined scope to support efficient planning and
execution. Nicole discussed the packet of information that was handed out
indicating that the packet provided to the Supervisors includes all
necessary documentation for the Navajo Road Project N8065, such as
scope of work, cost estimates, material quotes, and supporting letters. She
stated that for projects with Navajo Nation without vendor quotes,
documentation is included to show due diligence. She further stated that
larger or more complex projects may also require resolutions from local
governing entities or chapters, especially for partnership efforts. Nicole
stated that it is important to prioritize projects that can realistically be
completed within the first three years of procurement timelines. Nicole
noted that a valid and current IGA (Intergovernmental Agreement) is
critical, as delays in renewing or signing it, especially if legal review is
needed, can push projects into the next fiscal year and impact others.
Rochelle indicated that we currently have IGA’s in place with Fort Apache
Agency BIA, WMAT, NDOT and Western Agency BIA and Chinle BIA/Fort
Defiance. Rochelle stated that we are waiting for the IGA to be signed by
the Hopi Chairman. Rochelle further stated that Nicole has been tasked
with being the steward of all these IGA’s so that we know their expiration
dates, so that we can start the renewal process early, ensuring it becomes
standard practice rather than a last-minute scramble. Rochelle indicated
that since gathering signatures from partner agencies can take time, early
action helps avoid delays. Rochelle thanked Nicole and stated that a major
achievement has been Nicole’s success in getting all IGAs updated and in
place, which reinforces and renews the county’s partnership with the tribal
community, including with the transit agency. Supervisor Benally asked if
there is an IGA in place with the Chinle Agency. Rochelle indicated that
  
                                                                    11 of 15 
DRAFT

<!-- page 18 -->
they would make sure and get back to her. Jeanine Carruthers indicated
that we do have an IGA in place with the Western Agency. Nicole stated
that in addition, all IGAs must be submitted to the Board of Supervisors for
approval before their first meeting in April to meet procurement deadlines.
She further stated that missing this deadline increases the risk of project
delays and the possibility of it being pushed into the next fiscal year.
Nicole indicated that it is important to remember that projects exceeding
the $100,000 threshold typically add two to three months to the project
timeline. She further indicated that equally, if not more important, is
understanding your partners’ priorities to ensure alignment and mutual
investment in projects. She stated that engaging with agency partners
about their goals helps build successful, collaborative efforts while
respecting the sovereignty of each department. She listed the key partners
as often included are the Navajo Department of Transportation, Hopi
Department of Transportation, White Mountain Apache Tribe Department
of Transportation, and the five BIA jurisdictions. Nicole wrapped up her
comments by reviewing the highly visible projects with immediate impacts,
examples include cost-sharing partnerships, where the county funds
materials and partner agencies contribute labor. She stated that these
would be projects such as culverts, gravel, or sign fabrication. She further
stated that another example is partnering to reimburse agencies for hiring
personnel, such as heavy equipment operators, to work on roads owned
by those agencies. Nicole stated that these collaborative efforts
demonstrate effective use of resources and strengthen interagency
relationships and visible results. Jeanine Carruthers spoke about the
county's bid process for contracts, noting that anything over $100,000 has
to go out for bid.  She explained the bid process, including preparing a
comprehensive bid package that follows specific statutes, publishing the
bid in the county newspaper, allowing 21-28 days for vendors to review
and download bid materials, publicly opening bids after the specified
period, the Board approval of the contract, and within ten days they have
to get the contract in place, including obtaining certificates of insurance, if
needed.  She indicated that the entire process takes about 8 weeks, and
for more technical projects like engineering services, it can take even
longer. She further indicated that the goal is to give vendors sufficient time
to review and prepare their bids while following legal requirements. 
Rochelle spoke about the importance of early communication and joint
priority setting between the county and tribal transportation departments. 
She indicated that the county priorities often don't align with transportation
department priorities, necessitating negotiations. She further indicated that
staff would like to start conversations early to prepare projects for Q2-Q3,
avoiding last-minute rushes and ensuring projects can be completed within
the fiscal year. She stated that by engaging with the Supervisors about
their district priorities now, they hope to prevent project rollovers and
maximize fund utilization. She reviewed the budgets specific to the
districts.  Supervisor Benally inquired about the rollover costs. 
Rochelle stated that rollover costs are projects that started in the current
fiscal year but would not be able to be finished in the current fiscal year,
so they are charged to the next fiscal year.  She continued to review the
district budget worksheets.  She spoke about the budget components,
including fixed costs, rollover projects, and proposed new projects.

Meeting Date: 07/15/2025  
Title: DEMA Border Fencing and Technology Grant Agreement M25-0024
Submitted For: David Clouse, Sheriff 
Submitted By: Kimberley Willis, Sheriff's Office Finance Manager
Department: Sheriff 
Motion before the Board:
Department of Emergency and Military Affairs (DEMA) Border Fencing and Technology Grant
Agreement M25-0024 for additional funding for equipment to be utilized for Operation Desert Guardian
Background:
The Navajo County Sheriff's Office, in conjunction with Coconino, Gil, and Apache County, have
partnered to support the mission of Operation Desert Guardian. The operation will consist of local
neighborhood criminal suppression details, interstate criminal interdiction details, and tactical operations
throughout the State of Arizona. The Navajo County Sheriff's Office requested and was approved for
$372,230 in equipment and $119,158 in supplies and training to further this effort. The total of the award
from the Department of Emergency and Military Affairs (DEMA) is $419.388 and there is no required
match. Equipment and supplies include, but are not limited to: ballistic shields, 2 upfitted vehicles, xray,
LPRs, night vision goggles, pepperball guns, and bola wraps.

Title: ACJC Residential Substance Abuse Treatment FY 2026 Award
Submitted For: David Clouse, Sheriff 
Submitted By: Kimberley Willis, Sheriff's Office Finance Manager
Department: Sheriff 
Motion before the Board:
Arizona Criminal Justice Commission (ACJC) FY 2026 Residential Substance Abuse Treatment award
Background:
The Navajo County Sheriff's Office has once again been awarded funds by the Arizona Criminal Justice
Commission (ACJC) through the Residential Substance Abuse Treatment Program. The Navajo County
Sheriff's Office, in collaboration with Wextford Health (its existing contracted jail medical provider), has
developed a jail-based 90- day substance use disorder (SUD) treatment program. The award allows
$200,000 for professional consultant & contractual services for funding of the SUD treatment services
program, including staffing, management fees, and program costs. Additionally, $40,000 was awarded
for temporary housing and moving costs to fund costs for up to 80 individuals lodging in a local
motel/hotel to help assist them in making a successful transition into the community. The total of this
award is $240,000 with $180,000 in federal funding from the US Department of Justice, Office of Justice
Programs, and a $60,000 required grant match slated to pay out of the Opioid Settlement Money received
by this Office. The term of this grant award is from July 1, 2025 through June 30, 2026.

Title: C&A of the Silver Creek Flood Protection Dist. and appoint
Submitted By: Rayleen Richards, Elections Director
Department: Elections
Motion before the Board:
Resolution Number 18–2025, canceling the October 1, 2025, Election for the Silver Creek Flood
Protection District and appointing two (2) positions and deeming one (1) position vacant
Background:
48-1082. Elected board of directors; initial members; qualifications; terms; filling vacancy;
chairperson
E. If only one person files or no person files a nominating petition for election to fill a
position on the board of directors of the district, the county board of supervisors, by
resolution, may cancel the election for that office and appoint the person who filed the
nominating petition to fill that position. If no person files a nominating petition for an
election to fill a district board office, the county board of supervisors, by resolution, may
cancel the election for those offices and those offices are deemed vacant and shall be filled
as otherwise provided by law. A person who is appointed pursuant to this section is fully
vested with the powers and duties of the office as if elected to that office.

RESOLUTION NO ______ -25
A RESOLUTION OF THE NAVAJO COUNTY BOARD OF SUPERVISORS,
CANCEL THE OCTOBER 1, 2025, ELECTION FOR THE
SILVER CREEK FLOOD PROTECTION DISTRICT
PURSUANT TO ARS § 48-1082(E)
WHEREAS, ARS § 48-1082(E) provides as follows with respect to Flood Protection District elections:
“If only one person files or no person files a nominating petition for an election to fill a district office,
the board of supervisors may cancel the election for the position and appoint the person who filed
the nominating petition to fill the position.”
and
      WHEREAS, A.R.S. § 16-410 (A) authorizes the Board to take such action no earlier than one hundred and
five (105) days before the election, and it is now less than one hundred and five (105) days before the Oct. 1, 2025,
Election at which the Silver Creek Flood Protection District Board of Directors will be elected;
WHEREAS, the officer in charge of elections, has certified that two (2) persons have filed a nominating
petition to fill the Silver Creek Flood Protection District Board of Directors position(s), attached hereto and
incorporated herein as “Exhibit A”

Board of Supervisors Regular 1. m.        
Meeting Date: 07/15/2025  
Title: Approval of Legal Counsel for the PSPRS and CORP Boards, Contract B25-03-012
Submitted For: Lea Petersen, Deputy Commander 
Submitted By: Kolton Root, Sr. Procurement Officer
Department: Sheriff 
Motion before the Board:
Legal Counsel for the Public Safety Personnel Retirement System (PSPRS) and Corrections Officers
Retirement Plan (CORP) Boards, Contract #B25-03-012 with Ryan Rapp Pacheco Sorensen, PLC
Background:
Navajo County entered into agreements with the Public Safety Personnel Retirement System in 1979 and
the Corrections Officers Retirement Plan in 1987.  As a result, we have local PSPRS and CORP Boards.
When necessary, the Boards may seek legal counsel for various things such as personnel and employment
matters, laws, legislation, etc. The Boards are outside the jurisdiction of the Navajo County Attorney's
Office and therefore cannot receive legal counsel from the NCAO. It is also important for the Boards to
receive training regarding applicable laws and procedures. The consultant will provide these services to
the local PSPRS and CORP Boards as part of a one-year contract with the option to renew up to four
additional one-year terms.
Sealed proposals for this scope of work were due to the Board of Supervisors at the Navajo County
Governmental Center, Holbrook, Arizona by 3:00 pm (MST) April 30, 2025. Three firms were notified;
however, no proposals were received by the due date and time. The consultant currently providing these
services, Ryan Rapp Pacheco Sorensen, PLC., is willing and able to continue providing the same services
under the same terms. Staff recommend approval of the contract with Ryan Rapp Pacheco Sorensen,
PLC., as presented.

Chief Elected Officials and LWDB Partnership Agreement
I. PURPOSE
WHEREAS, Navajo County was designated by the Apache County, Navajo County, and Gila
County WIOA Partnership to serve as the Chief Elected Official, fiscal agent, and
administrative entity for the Northeastern Arizona Local Workforce Area.
WHEREAS, the INTERGOVERNMENTAL AGREEMENT for the Apache County, Navajo
County, and Gila County WIOA Partnership provides Navajo County the authority to
administer WIOA funds and to assign resources to meet the needs in each county.
WHEREAS, the Northeastern Arizona Local Workforce Development Board, hereinafter
referred to as LWDB, has been appointed by the Navajo County Board of Supervisors,
hereinafter referred to as Chief Elected Officials (CEO) as designated in the
INTERGOVERNMENTAL AGREEMENT, in accordance with the requirements in the
Workforce Innovation and Opportunity Act of 2014 (WIOA)
WHEREAS, the CEO AND LWDB PARTNERSHIP AGREEMENT is required by the Workforce
Arizona Council to acknowledge LWDB membership and to specify the relationship between
the CEO and LWDB.
II. LOCAL BOARD MEMBERSHIP
The WIOA  Section  107 (c)(l) authorizes  the CEO to appoint  the members  of the LWDB  in
accordance  with the criteria  established under  Section  107(b)(l)  of the WIOA  and the
guidelines in Workforce Arizona Council Policy 01­2019 entitled Workforce Innovation and
Opportunity Act (WIOA) Local Governance.
The CEO must appoint  business  representatives  who meet the criteria  established by  the
Workforce  Arizona  Council  on behalf of the Governor.
The authority to appoint members to the LWDB lies solely with the CEO.
A. Terms
LWDB members are appointed for a fixed and staggered 5­year term. Members may be
reappointed upon completion of their term.
B. Nomination
For each LWDB  member position that  requires  a nomination, the  nominating
organization as required by  the Workforce Innovation  and Opportunity  Act must
submit to the Navajo County Board of Supervisors as CEO of the local area a document

<!-- page 122 -->
Northeastern Arizona Local Workforce Development Board
pg. 2
or letter signed  by the chief executive  officer  or designee  identifying  the individual
being nominated . The document or letter must  also acknowledge the nominee's
optimum policy­making  authority  and include  documentation of  curriculum  vitae,
resume , or work  history  supporting  the qualifications  of the nomination. Multiple
individuals may  be nominated  for one LWDB position. The CEO shall make an
appointment  of one of the individuals  nominated in its role to appoint LWDB
members.
C. Appointment
LWDB appointments must be  made by the Navajo County Board of Supervisors  as
designated CEO. Navajo County will make appointments based on recommendations
received  from Navajo,  Apache,  and Gila Counties , and submit  them to the local
administrative  entity either  in the form of a letter,  evidenced within minutes  of
meetings, or other official communication. An email from the Navajo County Board of
Supervisors  or their  designee  will serve as one form of official  communication.
D. Change in Status
LWDB  members  who no longer  hold the position or  status that made them eligible
LWDB members must resign or be removed by the Navajo County Board of Supervisors
as designated CEO immediately upon notification to the LWDB Chair of the change of
status as  a representative  of that entity.
E. Mid-Term Appointment
LWDB members replacing outgoing mid­term members will serve the remainder of the
outgoing  member's  term.
F. Vacancies
A minimum of three business representatives from each county will serve on the LWDB.
The remaining  business  seats will be  filled by  a representative  from a different county
every two  years to ensure  continued equitable representation  from each county.  That
balance  in LWDB  representation  shall be maintained  when filling vacancies.
Recommended  nominees  shall be  submitted by  the appropriate  organizations to the
Navajo County Board of Supervisors, as designated CEO, for formal appointment.
LWDB vacancies  must be filled within  120 days of the vacancy.  Navajo  County
Board of Supervisors,  as designated CEO,  is authorized  to make all reappointments  of
members. Reappointments  must be made within  120 days of the term’s  expiration. In
the event  a vacancy  cannot  be filled within  120 days, the CEO  must request  a waiver
in writing  to the Director  of the Workforce  Arizona  Council  with an explanation  of
why a vacancy was not  filled in  the 120­day timeframe  and a description of  the
process underway to fill  the vacancy . The CEO must maintain  written  approval of  the
waiver  request  by the Director  of the Workforce  Arizona  Council  and be monitored
according  to the process outlined in their approved waiver request.
G. Removal
LWDB members must be removed by the Navajo County Board of Supervisors, as
designated  CEO, if any of the following  occurs :

<!-- page 123 -->
Northeastern Arizona Local Workforce Development Board
pg. 3
a) Documented violation of conflict of interest.

<!-- page 124 -->
Northeastern Arizona Local Workforce Development Board
pg. 4
b) Failure  to meet  LWDB  representation  requirements  defined  in the WIOA  and
Workforce Arizona Council policy.
c) Documented  proof of malfeasance,  fraud, or abuse.
In addition,  LWDB  members  may be removed from  the LWDB  for cause, as outlined in
the LWDB  bylaws , if a two­thirds (2/3)  majority  of the LWDB  approves  such actions  at
a legally constituted meeting . Such action must be confirmed by  a four­fifths (4/5)
majority  of the Navajo County Board of  Supervisors,  as designated CEO.
Ill. RELATIONSHIP BETWEEN CEO AND THE LWDB
The Navajo  County  Board of  Supervisors,  as designated CEO, provides  the following
guidance to the LWDB.  In the case  of required partnership or approval by  the CEO, the
LWDB  shall get in writing.  either through official communication  or evidence  in minutes
of meetings, evidence  of Chief Elected Official involvement
The LWDB  shall in accordance  with an Agreement  with the Chief  Elected  Officials  and
according  to the Workforce Innovation and  Opportunity Act  legislative requirements:
A In partnership  with the Chief Elected  Officials,  LWDB  Shall:
Develop  and submit  a local workforce business plan  consistent  with WIOA,  Arizona
State Integrated Plan, Workforce Arizona Council , and other state  administrative
entity requirements.  The local workforce  development business plan is developed in
partnership  with the LWDB,  the CEO, the Core Partners,  and the required partners
identified in  WIOA.  LWDB  Staff shall convene  key stakeholders  and oversee  the
local workforce business plan development process to ensure input from  all partners
and consensus  on strategy  and service delivery . Upon completion of the  draft
workforce development plan, it shall be submitted for  review  and approval  by the
LWDB  and the CEO.
B. Approval  required  by the Chief Elected  Officials:
Develop  a budget  for carrying  out the duties of the LWDB.
The LWDB will develop an operating budget for  carrying  out the duties  of the board,
which is approved  by the CEO. LWDB  staff develop  a draft operating  budget  for each
program/fiscal  year that includes  line items  for carrying out  the duties of the board and
allocating funds for service  delivery. Once the  draft budget is developed, it is submitted
to the LWDB  and CEO for review  and discussion;  any required changes  are made  by
staff and then  submitted to  the LWDB  and CEO for final approval.  LWDB  staff
provides  LWDB with monthly updates on year ­to­date spending progress . Any
revisions to the original  budget  are resubmitted  to the LWDB  and CEO for approval.
As part of the annual  budget  development  process,  the LWDB  and t h e  CEO will

<!-- page 125 -->
Northeastern Arizona Local Workforce Development Board
pg. 5
negotiate

<!-- page 126 -->
Northeastern Arizona Local Workforce Development Board
pg. 6
the amount to be paid by LWDB to Navajo County for its performance of the
Administrative and Fiscal Agent role outlined in the INTERGOVERNMENTAL
AGREEMENT. The most recent Navajo County 0MB Circular A­87 Cost Allocation
Plan will be used for those charges; however, due to the availability of funding, Navajo
County recognizes that it may be necessary to charge the LWDB less than is indicated
by the Cost Allocation Plan. In that case, Navajo County agrees to supplement any
actual administrative overhead costs with non ­federal funding.
C. Selection  of operators  and providers.
The LWDB  has established  guidelines  and a Request  for Proposal process  for the
selection  of operators  and providers in  the local  area in accordance with  WIOA
107(d)(10)(A) through (E) and the Workforce Arizona Council policy, including the
CEO's agreement  on the selections. Navajo  County,  as the administrative  and fiscal
agent, conducts  all procurements at  the request  of the LWDB  according  to the
county's  procurement  policy. An ad­hoc  work group  of LWDB  members  and staff is
convened  to complete  the proposal review , scoring,  and recommendation  of the top
respondent . The Navajo County Finance Director facilitates the proposal review and
scoring process. The recommendation  is presented to  the full LWDB  for approval .
Upon approval  by the LWDB,  a contract  is drafted  and presented to  the CEO for
agreement  and signature .
D. Youth activities
The LWDB  does not have a standing  youth committee.  The LWDB  has retained  the
responsibility  to select providers  and oversee  the youth activities  under youth formula
programs.  The LWDB will carry out its responsibilities  for youth activities under  youth
formula  funds by developing  a Youth Program  Design  framework  that includes  an
outreach and marketing plan,  career pathway models  for in­demand and emerging
industries and occupations, partnerships with  community­based organizations that
serve young  adults with barriers to  employment, and a youth participant framework
tool for ARIZONA@WORK partner staff. The Youth Program service provider will be
responsible  for the implementation  of the Youth Program Design  framework.
E. Program oversight
The LWDB provides oversight of the Adult, Dislocated Worker, and Youth programs.
Regularly  scheduled  monitoring  will be conducted by  local board staff. Service
providers  will provide  monthly  reports  to LWDB  staff. The One Stop Operator  will
coordinate with LWDB  staff and service  providers to  provide quarterly reports  to the
LWDB on the performance of the ARIZONA@WORK Northeastern Arizona System. The
One Stop Operator reports  will include updates  on activities and performance  of the
Adult, Dislocated Worker, and Youth Programs. LWDB Staff will provide quarterly
budget  updates  to include  expenditure reports  for the Adult, Dislocated Worker,  and
Youth programs.  Service  provider  management  staff will attend the quarterly  LWDB

<!-- page 127 -->
Northeastern Arizona Local Workforce Development Board
pg. 7
meetings to  answer any  questions that  may arise from the  One Stop Operator and/or
LWDB Staff reports. At the annual joint LWBD/CEO board meeting, the local board staff
will provide  a monitoring  report to the LWDB  and CEO.
F. Performance accountability measures
The LWDB and CEO are responsible for performance accountability measures and give
authority  to LWDB  staff to negotiate  performance  measures.  After performance
measures  have been negotiated,  the LWDB  staff will provide  details  to the LWDB
and CEO.
G. Local Board policy
The CEO is involved in overall strategic direction of the LWDB and designates
responsibility to the LWDB to develop local board policy. Local workforce policies shall
be developed by the LWDB Staff and Executive Committee and presented to the full
LWDB for input and final approval.
H. LWDB Bylaws.
The Bylaws  may be amended  or repealed  in part or in whole only by a majority  vote
of the LWDB  members  at a regular  or special meeting  with a quorum present
L Memorandum of Understanding
The LWDB, with the agreement of the CEO, shall develop and enter into a Memorandum
of Understanding (between the LWDB  and each of the partners),  concerning  the
operation  of the one­stop  delivery  system in the  local area:
a) In coordination with the LWDB, the board staff shall develop the Memorandum
of Understanding with the core partners;
b) The LWDB shall review and approve the Memorandums of Understanding;
c) The Chair of the LWDB shall sign the Memorandums of Understanding; and
d) The LWDB will submit the Memorandums of Understanding to the CEO for
approval and signature.
J. Other LWDB roles and responsibilities:
a) Select a private­sector business representative as LWDB chairperson; and
b) Create  an annual  report that must be submitted to the Workforce
Arizona Council  as per guidelines  established  by the Workforce
Arizona  Council; and
c) Promote one­stop center programs and activities;  and
d) Assist the state administrative  entity in developing  the statewide
employment statistics system under the  Wagner Peyser Act;  and

<!-- page 128 -->
Northeastern Arizona Local Workforce Development Board
pg. 8
e) Coordinate  with economic  development  strategies  and establish
employer linkages  with workforce development activities; and
t) Carry out regional planning  responsibilities  as required by  the
Workforce  Arizona Council and the  state administrative  entity, and the
Workforce Investment  Act in Section  116 (c); and
g) Per WIOA  Regulations  679.370,  promote  private  sector involvement  in the
statewide workforce  investment system through effective connecting,
brokering,  and coaching  activities through intermediaries,  such as the one­
stop operator in the  local area,  the local business  services function , or
through  other organizations to  assist employers  in meeting  hiring needs;
and
h) Conduct  business  in an open manner by  making  available to the  public
information  about the activities  of the LWDB;  this includes  the local  plan
before  submission, membership,  designation  of the local workforce
system operator, the awarding of grants or contracts, and minutes of
LWDB meetings; and
i) Ensure  that partner  staff will be governed by  their respective
personnel laws  and collective bargaining  agreements  in a One­Stop
Center.
Other LWDB Requirements:
Authority to Hire LWDB Staff: WIOA sec. 107(f) grants the LWDB authority to hire a
director  and other staff to assist in carrying  out the functions  of the LWDB  in agreement
with the CEO, since Navajo  County  is the employer  of record.
1. LWDBs must establish and apply a set of qualifications  for the position of director
that ensures  the individual selected has  the requisite knowledge,  skills, and abilities  to
meet identified  benchmarks  and to assist in carrying  out the functions  of the LWDB.
2. The LWDB  director  and staff must be subject  to the limitations  on the payment  of
salary and bonuses described in  WIOA  sec. 194(15).
3. In general, LWDB staff may only assist the LWDB to fulfill its functions (20 CFR
679.400 (d)).
Conflict  of Interest:
The Navajo County Board of Supervisors, in partnership with the LWDB, have designated to
their respective  staff the development of  an Organizational  Chart depicting the  staff that
perform  the governance  functions  and the associated  chain of command , as well as the
staff that  perform the  operational  functions  and the associated chain of  command.  The
Organizational  Chart is attached to this  document  as Exhibit  "A".
The designation  of different  departments  within  Navajo  County  and the associated  chains

<!-- page 129 -->
Northeastern Arizona Local Workforce Development Board
pg. 9
of command  creates  the firewalls  required by  WIOA  and corresponding  regulations .

<!-- page 130 -->
Northeastern Arizona Local Workforce Development Board
pg. 10
Uniform Guidance (2 CFR  part 200), the State's  conflict  of interest  policy (20 CFR
679.430 and  State Workforce Policy  #8), State Workforce  Policy  #4
(ARIZONA@WORK  One Stop Operator and Service Provider Selection), and State
Workforce Policy #1  (Local Governance).
The following description of duties and chains of command  shall identify a clear separation
of duties  and  required firewalls  as required by  20 CFR  679.430.
LWDB Staff:
LWDB Staff shall perform those duties  associated with the LWDB 's governance and
oversight role.  Specifically,  LWDB  staff is responsible for  supporting  the Local
Workforce Development Board in performing  its Roles  and Responsibilities:  As stated
in WIOA  sec. 107(d), 20 CFR 679 .370, and 20 CFR 679.310 (b),  the LWDB must:
1. Local Policy:  In partnership  with the CEO, the LWDB  sets policy for the
portion  of the statewide  workforce development system  within  the LWDA  and
consistent with State policies (20  CFR 679.310 ( b)).
2. Local Plan:  Develop  and submit  a 4­year  local plan for the LWDA,  in
partnership with the CEO  and consistent with  WIOA  section  108 (20  CFR
679.370(a)); If  the LWDA is  part of a planning  region that  includes  other
LWDAs  as designated by  the Governor under 20  CFR 679.200  and 679 .210, the
LWDB in partnership with the CEO must  develop and submit  a regional plan in
collaboration with the LWDBs and CEOs from other  areas. (WIOA  106 (c)(2)
and 20  CFR 679.510) . The regional plan must  incorporate the  local plans from
each LWDA  in the planning  region  per 20  CFR
679.540  (a).
3. Labor Market Analysis: To assist in the development and implementation of the
local  plan,  conduct  workforce  research  and  regional  labor  market  analysis  to
include:
a. Analyses  and  regular  updates  of economic  conditions,  needed
knowledge  and skills, workforce, and workforce development (including
education and training) activities  to include  an analysis  of the strengths  and
weaknesses (including  the capacity  to provide)  of such  services  to address
the identified  education  and  skill needs  of the workforce and the
employment needs  of employers;
b. Assistance  to the Governor  in developing  the statewide  workforce
and  labor market  information  system under  the Wagner­Peyser Act  for
the region;
c. Other  research,  data  collection,  and analysis  related  to the  workforce  needs
of the regional  economy  as the LWDB,  after  receiving  input  from  a wide

<!-- page 131 -->
Northeastern Arizona Local Workforce Development Board
pg. 11
array  of stakeholders, determines to be necessary to  carry  out  its functions .
4. Convening, Brokering, Leveraging: Convene the local workforce development
system
stakeholders to  assist in the development of  the local plan under WIOA
section  108 and 20  CFR 679.550  and in identifying non­Federal expertise and
resources  to leverage  support for  workforce development activities.  Such
stakeholders may assist the LWDB and standing committees in carrying out,
convening, brokering, and leveraging  functions  at the direction  of the LWDB .
5. Employer Engagement: Lead efforts to engage with a diverse range of
employers and other entities in the  region in order to :
a. Promote business representation  (particularly representatives  with
optimum policymaking  or hiring authority  from employers whose
employment opportunities reflect  existing  and emerging  employment
opportunities  in the region)  on the LWDB;
b. Develop  effective  linkages  (including  the use of intermediaries)  with
employers in  the region  to support  employer  utilization  of the local
workforce  development system and to  support local  workforce investment
activities;
c. Ensure  that workforce  investment  activities  meet the needs  of
employers  and support  economic  growth in the region by  enhancing
communication, coordination,  and collaboration among  employers,
economic  development entities, and service providers; and
d. Develop  and implement  proven  or promising  strategies  for meeting
the employment  and skill needs of workers  and employers (such  as
the establishment of  industry and  sector partnerships),  that provide
the skilled workforce  needed  by employers  in the region,  and that
expand  employment and career  advancement opportunities for
workforce development system participants in in­demand industry
sectors or occupations.
6. Career Pathways Development: With representatives  of secondary  and
post­ secondary education programs,  lead efforts to develop  and
implement career pathways within the LWDA by aligning the employment,
training, education, and supportive  services  that are needed by  adults and
youth,  particularly  individuals with  barriers to  employment as  defined in
WIOA 3(24) .
7. Proven  and Promising  Practices:  Lead efforts  in the LWDA  to identify
and promote  proven  and promising  strategies  and initiatives  for meeting  the

<!-- page 132 -->
Northeastern Arizona Local Workforce Development Board
pg. 12
needs of employers, workers  and jobseekers (including individuals with
barriers to employment) in  the local workforce  system,  as well as in
providing  physical  and programmatic accessibility,  in accordance with
section  188, if applicable,  and applicable provisions  of the Americans  with
Disabilities  Act of 1990 (42 U.S.C. 12101  et seq.), to the one­stop delivery
system.
4. Technology: Develop strategies for using technology to maximize accessibility  and
effectiveness  of the local workforce development  system for employers,  workers , and job
seekers , by:
a. Facilitating connections  among the intake and case management
information systems of the  one­stop partner programs to support  a
comprehensive workforce  development  system  in the LWDA
b. Facilitating  access  to services  provided  through  the one­stop  delivery
system involved , including access in remote areas
c. Identifying  strategies  for better meeting  the needs of individuals  with barriers
to employment, including  strategies  that augment traditional service  delivery,
and increase  access to services  and programs of  the one­stop  delivery  system,
such as improving digital literacy skills; and
d. Leveraging resources and capacity within the local workforce development
system , including resources  and capacity  for services for  individuals
with barriers to employment
1. Program Oversight: Provide program oversight, in partnership with the CEO, for
the LWDA:
a. Conduct  oversight of  youth workforce investment activities authorized  under
WIOA  section  129(c),  adult and dislocated worker employment and training
activities  under WIOA  sections  134 (c) and (d); and the entire one­stop
delivery system  in the LWDA;  and
b. Ensure  the appropriate  use and management  of the funds provided  under
WIOA subtitle B  for the youth,  adult, and dislocated worker  activities and
one­stop delivery  system  in the LWDA;  and
c. Ensure  the appropriate use,  management,  and investment of  funds to
maximize performance outcomes under  WIOA  section  116.
2. Local Performance Accountability  Measures: Negotiate and  reach an
agreement on local  performance  measures  with the CEO and the Governor  (WIOA
section  116 (c).
3. Infrastructure  Costs:  Negotiate with  the CEO and required partners  on the
methods for funding  the infrastructure  costs of one­stop  centers  in the LWDA  in
accordance with  § 678.715 and State Workforce Policy #5 MOU and Infrastructure Costs
policy.

<!-- page 133 -->
Northeastern Arizona Local Workforce Development Board
pg. 13
4. Selection of One Stop Operators and Providers: With the agreement of the CEO
for the LWDA, the LWDB selects the following providers in the LWDA, and where
appropriate , terminates  such providers in  accordance with 2  CFR part  200:
a. Providers  of youth work  investment activities through competitive grants  or
contracts  based on the recommendations  of the youth standing  committee  (if
such a committee is established). However, if the LWDB determines there is an
insufficient number of eligible providers of youth workforce investment activities in an
LWDA, the LWDB may award grants or contracts on a sole­source basis in compliance with
State Workforce Policy #4 ARIZONA@WORK One Stop Operator and Service Provider
Selection Policy. The LWDB may terminate for cause the eligibility of such providers.
b. Eligible providers  of training  services  consistent with the  criteria  and
information requirements established by  the Governor  and WIOA  sec.122;
c. Providers  of career services through the  award of  contracts  in compliance
with State Workforce Policy #4 ARIZONA@WORK One Stop Operator and
Service Provider Selection Policy, if the one­stop operator does not provide such
services.
d. Designate  or certify  one­stop operators  through a competitive process  as
described  in WIOA  section  121(d)(2)(A),  20 CFR 678.600  ­ 678.635,  and
State Workforce Policy #4 ARIZONA@WORK One Stop Operator and Service
Provider Selection Policy. Also, the LWDB may terminate for cause the
eligibility of such operators.
e. Consumer  Choice  Requirements: Work  with the  State to ensure  there are
sufficient numbers  and types  of providers of  career services  and training  services
serving  the LWDA,  services  are provided  in a manner  that maximizes  consumer
choice  and provides  opportunities that  lead to  competitive integrated employment
for individuals with  disabilities.  This includes  eligible providers  with expertise  in
assisting  individuals with  disabilities  and eligible providers with expertise  in
assisting adults in need of adult education and literacy activities. (WIOA section 122
and paragraphs (2) and (3) of section  134(c))
5. Coordination with Education Providers: Coordinate activities with education and
training providers in the LWDA, including:
a. Reviewing  applications  to provide  adult education  and literacy  activities  under
Title II for the LWDA  to determine  whether  such applications  are consistent  with
the local plan;
b. Making  recommendations  to the eligible  agency to  promote  alignment  with
such plan;  and

<!-- page 134 -->
Northeastern Arizona Local Workforce Development Board
pg. 14
c. Replicating  and implementing  cooperative  agreements to  enhance the
provision of services to  individuals with disabilities  and other individuals,  such
as cross­training  of staff, technical  assistance , use and sharing  of information,
cooperative efforts with  employers, and other  efforts  at cooperation,
collaboration,  and coordination.  Cooperative  agreement  means  an agreement
entered  into by a State­designated  agency  or State­designated  unit under
subparagraph  (A) of section lOl(a)(ll) of the  Rehabilitation Act of  1973.
6. Budget  and  Administration:  Develop  a budget  for the activities  of the LWDB,
with approval of the CEO, and consistent with the local plan and the duties of the LWDB.
The LWDB budget includes all activities of the LWDB, including the Title I budget
amounts to be allocated for youth (section  133)  and  adult  and dislocated worker
(section  128) career  services . The  LWDB  determines  how  much  of the budget  to
allocate  for these services  and how  to procure  these  services.  Budget  Disbursal:
The  local  grant  recipient or an entity  designated as the grant recipient shall
disburse  the grant  funds  for workforce  investment  activities  at the  direction  of the
LWDB,  pursuant  to the requirements  of WIOA.  The  local  grant  recipient  or entity
designated  as grant  recipient shall disburse the funds immediately on receiving such
direction from the LWDB (WIOA sec 107  (d)(12)(B)( i)(III)).
7. Accessibility for Individuals  with  Disabilities:  Assess,  on an annual
basis,  the physical  and programmatic  accessibility  of all one­stop  centers  in
the LWDA,  in accordance with  WIOA  section  188,  if applicable,  and
applicable provisions  of the Americans  with  Disabilities  Act  of 1990  (42
U.S.C. 12101et  seq.).
8. ARIZONA@WORK Job Center Certification: Certification of
ARIZONA@WORK job centers in  accordance with  20 CFR  § 678.800 and State
Workforce  Policy #6 Certification  of the ARIZONA@WORK Job  Centers.
LWDB staff report to the LWDB Executive Director. The Executive Director functionally
reports  to the  LWDB  and,  according to  the attached organizational  chart,  formally
reports  to the Navajo County Chief Financial Officer . LWDB Staff have a separate
reporting structure from  program  staff  and  do not  have  supervision  or control  over  the
Program  Staff.
Adult, Dislocated Worker, and Youth Program Provider Staff: Program provider staff
shall  only  perform  those  duties  associated  with  the direct  provision  of the Adult,
Dislocated Worker, and Youth Programs. Staff will not perform any roles listed above as
LWDB staff duties.
Program  staff  report  to the assigned Managed for contracted Service Provider, as evidenced on
the attached  organizational  chart,  the reporting  structure  for the contracted Service
provider has a distinct ly different  chain  of command from  the LWDB  Executive
Director.
Fiscal Agent: The Navajo County Chief Financial Officer serves as the Fiscal Agent for

<!-- page 135 -->
Northeastern Arizona Local Workforce Development Board
pg. 15
the LWDB. Navajo  County  has one financial  system  that  operates  a fund  accounting
system . Separate funds  have  been  established  for Program  Staff  to track  expenditures .
Program  staff  do not have access to the overall budget and funds associated with the LWDB.
The LWDB Admin Officer  serves  as staff  to the LWDB,  preparing  and  submitting
expenditure reports  and requests  for reimbursement  from  the Arizona Department  of
Economic  Security.  The LWDB A d m i n  O f f i c e r  has access to view the Program Staff
funds for oversight and monitoring purposes.
IV. AMENDMENTS/CHANGE TO AGREEMENT/ELECTION
This AGREEMENT may be amended or changed with a written notice by the
CEO to the LWDB. Election notice of a new LWDB Chair or Chief Elected
Official will be maintained in the Administrative Entity office.
V. MAINTENANCE OF AGREEMENT
The original AGREEMENT and any modifications shall be maintained and
available  for monitoring  at the Navajo  County  Administrative  Entity
office.
VI. COMMUNICATION
LWDB meeting notices and minutes of meetings will be provided to the Navajo,
Apache, and Gila County Boards of Supervisors. Navajo County Board of
Supervisors, in its official capacity as CEO of the local workforce area, will be
invited  to a joint meeting  with the LWDB  annually , wherein performance
information and financial information will be  shared.
Apache County and Gila County Boards of Supervisors, or their designees, will
also be  invited for  the purpose of updating  them on the performance and
activities  for the local workforce  area. The LWDB  will make available  to the
public,  on a regular  basis through open meetings,  information  regarding the
activities  of the LWDB.
VII. JOINT REPRESENTATION
The parties to this  agreement have  agreed to joint  representation  in this matter
by the Navajo  County Attorney's Office. Both parties  understand the
advantages and disadvantages of joint  representation,  including the  potential
loss of confidentiality,  and have determined that  the advantages of joint
representation outweigh the risks  and hereby consent to joint  representation.
